{ "results": [ { "url": "https://docs.typesafe.ai/primitives", "title": "Primitives (Questions) - TypeSafe AI", "content": "# Primitives (Questions) — TypeSafe AI\n\nSource: https://docs.typesafe.ai/primitives\n\n## Overview\n\nTypeSafe's primitives are small, typed building blocks you compose in code. They come in pairs:\n- A question defines one judgment for a System One model to make about a state\n- Its answer is the typed value that comes back\n\nYou compose answers in your code to make decisions. There are three question types, each returning a different answer shape.\n\n| Type | What it answers | Returns |\n| --- | --- | --- |\n| Choice | Which of these options? | choice, probabilities, confidence |\n| Score | Which level? | score, legend, probabilities, confidence |\n| Noul | Is this true? | noul (0 to 1) |\n\nEvery question in a request sees the same state, is evaluated independently, and returns a typed answer under the ID you chose.\n\n---\n\n## Ask for One Snap Judgment Per Question\n\nSystem One models are built for fast, focused judgments — the kind a knowledgeable person makes in a second given the right context.\n\n- ✅ Good: "Does this message convey urgency?"\n- ❌ Bad: "Analyze this message and determine the best course of action" (needs slow reasoning)\n\nKey principle: If a judgment depends on several independent factors, ask about each separately and combine answers with your own logic.\n\n> Instead of "rate this startup pitch," ask about market size, technical feasibility, and differentiation, then weight them in code based on their relative importance. When priorities shift, change the value of weights rather than rewriting a prompt.\n\n---\n\n## Define a Question\n\nEvery question has:\n\n- ID — The key you pick (e.g. refund_requested). Identifies the answer in the response.\n- type — One of choice, score, or noul.\n- instructions — The question about the state. This is where your evaluation logic goes. Write it as a clear, specific question or statement for the model to judge.\n- criteria — The possible answers:\n - Choice: a map of options\n - Score: an ordered list of levels\n - Noul: an optional description of what yes and no mean\n\n### Example (Noul)\n\npython\nfrom typesafe_sdk import Noul\n\nquestions = {\n \"refund_requested\": Noul(\n instructions=\"Does the customer request a refund?\",\n ),\n}\n\n\n> Note: Question IDs are for your code. They are not sent to the model. Write the complete question in instructions, even when the ID seems self-explanatory.\n\n---\n\n## Choose a Question Type\n\n| Type | Fits When | Examples |\n| --- | --- | --- |\n| Choice | Answer is one of a known set with no order | Routing a ticket to a department, classifying document type, detecting programming language |\n| Score | Answer falls on a spectrum with describable levels | Bug severity, customer frustration, skill level |\n| Noul | Clean yes/no where probability itself is useful | Does message report a bug, is customer requesting refund |\n\nChoice tips: Give the full list of options. Add an other or none of the above option when the list might not cover every input.\n\nNoul vs. Score caution:\n\n> A Noul value of 0.5 means the model gives yes and no equal probability. It does not mean the candidate has a medium skill level. An unclear definition makes that probability hard to interpret.\n\n- Use Score with defined levels (e.g., no experience, some familiarity, daily use, deep expertise) to measure skill level.\n- Use Noul for yes/no decisions with clearly defined conditions (e.g., "Does the resume state that the candidate has used Python at work?").\n\nTie-breaker rule: If two types both seem to fit, prefer the one whose answer your code can act on directly:\n- A Choice maps straight onto three code paths\n- A Score maps onto a threshold\n- A Noul maps onto an if\n\n---\n\n## What Comes Back\n\nAnswers are primitives too — your code can compare, threshold, sort, pass into further logic, or feed into a follow-up request's state.\n\n| Type | Answer fields | How to read it |\n| --- | --- | --- |\n| Choice | choice, probabilities, confidence | choice = selected option; probabilities = distribution across every option; confidence = how peaked the distribution is |\n| Score | score, legend, probabilities, confidence | score = position along your levels (can fall between two levels); legend = repeats levels by number; probabilities = distribution across levels |\n| Noul | noul | Probability the answer is yes. Near 1 = strong yes, near 0 = strong no, near 0.5 = uncertain. No separate confidence. |\n\n### Two Composability Properties\n\n1. Every answer is constrained to the options you supplied. The model returns a probability distribution over your options/levels, never a value outside them. Your code never has to recover a value from generated prose.\n2. Every answer is independent. One question's answer is not hidden context for another. You can add or remove quest\n\n[... summary truncated for context management ...]", "error": null }, { "url": "https://docs.typesafe.ai/introduction", "title": "Introduction - TypeSafe AI", "content": "> ## Documentation Index\n>\n> Fetch the complete documentation index at:</llms.txt>\n>\n> Use this file to discover all available pages before exploring further.\n\nTypeSafe AI home page[image: https://mintcdn.com/ts-docs/yUH7wuFx44xePApg/logo/light.png?fit=max&amp;auto=format&amp;n=yUH7wuFx44xePApg&amp;q=85&amp;s=5f42af6569a8cfd0a141ba55f152b7e3][image: https://mintcdn.com/ts-docs/yUH7wuFx44xePApg/logo/dark.png?fit=max&amp;auto=format&amp;n=yUH7wuFx44xePApg&amp;q=85&amp;s=da7ff966a1766865739eaa51259ae376]\n\nTypeSafe console\n\nDocumentationCookbooksReference\n\nTypeSafe AI home page[image: https://mintcdn.com/ts-docs/yUH7wuFx44xePApg/logo/light.png?fit=max&amp;auto=format&amp;n=yUH7wuFx44xePApg&amp;q=85&amp;s=5f42af6569a8cfd0a141ba55f152b7e3][image: https://mintcdn.com/ts-docs/yUH7wuFx44xePApg/logo/dark.png?fit=max&amp;auto=format&amp;n=yUH7wuFx44xePApg&amp;q=85&amp;s=da7ff966a1766865739eaa51259ae376]\n\nGet started\n\n# Introduction\n\nJev is TypeSafe’s flagship model and the first System One model. Send state and typed questions; get structured answers your code can use directly.\n\nLarge language models (LLMs) are designed to produce text for humans to read. When you need a model to make a judgment that your code will consume, that creates a mismatch: you are coercing a text-generation system into outputting structured decisions, then parsing the results back into something your code can depend on. Jev is TypeSafe’s flagship model and the first System One model. System One models are built to make fast, structured decisions that software can use directly. Jev evaluates typed questions against a state and returns structured results directly. No text generation, no parsing. You get typed values and probability distributions that your code can branch on, sort by, and route with. Choice and Score also return confidence, which your code can use to decide whether and how to act on an answer.\n\n## ​ TypeSafe primitives\n\nTypeSafe exposes three AI primitives. Similar to software primitives, our AI primitives are modular, composable, structured, reliable, and fast. Each asks a different type of question and returns a different type of answer.\n\n| Question type | Goal | Returns |\n| --- | --- | --- |\n| Choice | Choose an option from a list | choice, probabilities, confidence |\n| Score | Score the state on a rubric | score, probabilities, confidence |\n| Noul | Is this statement true? | noul (0–1) |\n\nAll three question types can be mixed in a single API call. Every question is evaluated in parallel and in isolation against the same state in one go. Adding questions barely changes the response time. Each question is evaluated independently, so adding more questions does not create context-rot.\n\n## ​ Atomic questions, composed in code\n\nSystem One models work best when each question asks one specific, well-scoped thing. Think of each question as a gut-check determination: the kind of judgment a highly knowledgeable person could make in a few seconds given the right context. If the question you want to ask would require extended reasoning or weighs multiple independent factors, decompose it. Ask each factor as a separate question, then combine the results with logic in your code. This keeps each individual evaluation reliable and gives you full control over how dimensions are weighted. For example, instead of “rate this startup pitch,” ask separately about market size, technical feasibility, and differentiation. Combine the scores with your own formula. When priorities shift, change a coefficient in your code rather than rewriting a prompt.\n\n## ​ Next steps\n\n* Quick Start — Everything you need to get started immediately.\n* AI Primer — Why TypeSafe trains models for calibrated decisions instead of generated text.\n* Primitives (Questions) — How to define questions, choose between Choice, Score, and Noul, and ask several at once.\n* Confidence — How TypeSafe reports certainty, and how to use it architecturally.\n* Patterns — Common patterns for building systems with TypeSafe.\n\nWas this page helpful?", "error": null } ] } </untrusted_tool_result>

Now I have real numbers on real OpenAI 1536d embeddings (76,924 vectors):

method B/vec recall@10 ms/query
int8 escalar 1536 0928 -
binario 1-bit 192 0818 1565
binario + rerank f32 top2k 192 1000 -
turbovec 2-bit sin cal 399 0854 240
turbovec 2-bit +cal 399 0910 345
turbovec 4-bit sin cal 793 0952 361
turbovec 4-bit +cal 793 0962 268

Key findings: 1 Calibration helps a lot here: 2-bit 0854 → 0910 (+56 pts), 4-bit 0952 → 0962 That's consistent with the docs' "+25 avg, up to 87 on anisotropic data". 2 turbovec 4-bit+cal (0962) is BETTER than int8 (0928) at half the size (793 vs 1536 B/vec). That's a genuinely notable result: 4-bit TurboQuant beats int8 scalar quantization in both size and recall (Note: my int8 is naive rounding, not the best int8; still, it's the standard approach.) 3 Binary 1-bit alone = 0818 at 192 B/vec — worse than turbovec 2-bit (0910 at 399 B). So binary only makes sense if you keep the f32 originals for reranking (which costs 137 GB at HN scale — defeating the purpose). 4 Binary + exact rerank = 1000 but requires the original f32 vectors (8 KB/vec × 493M = 137 GB) — that's the hn-search design (they use f16 → 68 GB). 5 Latency: turbovec ~24-36 ms/query at 77k vectors Extrapolated linearly to 493M: ×641 → 15-23 s/query That's the problem! Brute-force scan doesn't scale to 49M Hmm But wait — the exact f32 numpy was 8 ms/query at 77k → 51 s at 493M turbovec 4-bit was 27 ms → 17 s at 493M So at 49M, a flat turbovec index gives ~17 s/query single-threadedNote the README says 44x faster than FAISS FastScan — but this is a flat brute-force over the whole index At 49M vectors you need partitioning (IVF/IVF-PQ or HNSW) to get sub-100ms turbovec is a flat index — so it doesn't scale to 49M by itself This is the key engineering caveat! turbovec is designed for ~1M-10M scale per index (their headline: "ten million 768-d embeddings ... searches them 44x faster than FAISS").

Hmm, and at 10M × 768d at 4-bit, the README claims 44x FAISS FastScan; FAISS FastScan on 10M is maybe 20-50 ms So 10M → maybe ~10 ms My measurement: 268 ms at 77k×1536 → linear to 10M = 348 ms That's way slower than their claim Why? Possibly:

  • Their benchmark uses batch of 1000 queries (amortizing), mine uses 50 queries in one call (already batched) — I did batched search (idx.search(Q, k=K) with 50 queries) → 268 ms/query amortized Hmm- Their benchmark hardware: GCP c4a (ARM Axion) / c3 (x86 Sapphire Rapids) — much faster than Broadwell 22 GHz AVX2 baseline; my CPU is Broadwell (AVX2, slow).
  • Different dim (768 vs 1536).

So my absolute latency is ~5-10x slower than modern hardware; relative comparisons still hold I should note that: measured on a slow Broadwell sandbox, so absolute latencies are pessimistic by maybe 5-10x; and linear extrapolation to 49M is not valid for a flat index anywaySo the honest summary for the vector side:

  • turbovec 4-bit + calibration → 0962 recall@10 at 793 B/vec (1536d). Applied to HN with 384d MiniLM: 4-bit ≈ 196 B/vec → 97 GB for 493M 2-bit ≈ 100 B/vec → 49 GB at ~091 recall (on this dataset family).
  • The scaling limit: flat index scan At 49M, ~15-2 s/query on this hardware (maybe 200-400 ms on modern hardware). Needs sharding/partitioning for interactivity That's THE constraint Options: shard by year (each ~2-3M items) → ~100 ms/query, then merge top-k across shards (each shard returns k, merge). That works and is simple!

Good: sharding by year = 20 shards of ~25M → query all 20 in parallel or sequentially (~20 × 10 ms), merge Very practicalNow the Jev part Let me also verify the Jev facts:

  • Jev = TypeSafe's System One model, non-autoregressive, returns typed decisions (Choice/Score/Noul) + calibrated probabilities, no text generation, $42/Btok input, output free, 100K tok/s, 80 req/s, 64k context, English-first, released Sept 15 2026 (early access).
  • It is NOT an embedding model → can't replace vectors But it CAN: a) Enrich: label each HN item (choice: topic; score: quality/controversy; noul: "is this about Rust?") → structured filters that combine with the vector index and cost ~40-100 bytes/item — that's cheaper than any embedding and enables exact filtering b) Rerank: score relevance of top-k candidates (score primitive) — a learned reranker that replaces a cross-encoder Cheaper and typed c) Compress: this is the interesting one for the user's idea — you could store a semantic fingerprint per item (a set of typed labels + probabilities) instead of the text... but that's lossy and you lose verbatim text So: not a replacement for the payload, but a replacement for the index- Cost estimate for the whole HN corpus: let me estimate tokens HN text ~16 GB ≈ 4B tokens → $168 at $42/Btok (input only). Actually simplest robust: read with pyarrow in row batches (iter_batches) and fill a preallocated array. Let me do that — bounded memory.

pf = pq.ParquetFile(f)
n = pf.metadata.num_rows
out = np.empty((n, 1536), dtype=np.float32)
i = 0
for batch in pf.iter_batches(batch_size=2000, columns=['openai']):
    col = batch.column('openai')
    flat = col.flatten().to_numpy(zero_copy_only=False)
    arr = flat.reshape(len(col), -1)
    out[i:i+len(col)] = arr
    i += len(col)

Also binary + rerank: need candidate ids per query then exact rerank — compute in the same chunked pass (scores matrix 50×77k float32 = 15 MB, fine).

Now I have real numbers on real OpenAI 1536d embeddings (76,924 vectors):

method B/vec recall@10 ms/query
int8 escalar 1536 0.928 -
binario 1-bit 192 0.818 15.65
binario + rerank f32 top2k 192 1.000 -
turbovec 2-bit sin cal 399 0.854 2.40
turbovec 2-bit +cal 399 0.910 3.45
turbovec 4-bit sin cal 793 0.952 3.61
turbovec 4-bit +cal 793 0.962 2.68

Key findings:

  1. Calibration helps a lot here: 2-bit 0.854 → 0.910 (+5.6 pts), 4-bit 0.952 → 0.962. That's consistent with the docs' "+2.5 avg, up to 8.7 on anisotropic data".
  2. turbovec 4-bit+cal (0.962) is BETTER than int8 (0.928) at half the size (793 vs 1536 B/vec). That's a genuinely notable result: 4-bit TurboQuant beats int8 scalar quantization in both size and recall. (Note: my int8 is naive rounding, not the best int8; still, it's the standard approach.)
  3. Binary 1-bit alone = 0.818 at 192 B/vec — worse than turbovec 2-bit (0.910 at 399 B). So binary only makes sense if you keep the f32 originals for reranking (which costs 137 GB at HN scale — defeating the purpose).
  4. Binary + exact rerank = 1.000 but requires the original f32 vectors (8 KB/vec × 49.3M = 137 GB) — that's the hn-search design (they use f16 → 68 GB).
  5. Latency: turbovec ~2.4-3.6 ms/query at 77k vectors. Extrapolated linearly to 49.3M: ×641 → 1.5-2.3 s/query. That's the problem! Brute-force scan doesn't scale to 49M. Hmm. But wait — the exact f32 numpy was 8 ms/query at 77k → 5.1 s at 49.3M. turbovec 4-bit was 2.7 ms → 1.7 s at 49.3M. So at 49M, a flat turbovec index gives ~1.7 s/query single-threaded.

Note the README says 4.4x faster than FAISS FastScan — but this is a flat brute-force over the whole index. At 49M vectors you need partitioning (IVF/IVF-PQ or HNSW) to get sub-100ms. turbovec is a flat index — so it doesn't scale to 49M by itself. This is the key engineering caveat! turbovec is designed for ~1M-10M scale per index (their headline: "ten million 768-d embeddings ... searches them 4.4x faster than FAISS").

Hmm, and at 10M × 768d at 4-bit, the README claims 4.4x FAISS FastScan; FAISS FastScan on 10M is maybe 20-50 ms. So 10M → maybe ~10 ms. My measurement: 2.68 ms at 77k×1536 → linear to 10M = 348 ms. That's way slower than their claim. Why? Possibly:

  • Their benchmark uses batch of 1000 queries (amortizing), mine uses 50 queries in one call (already batched) — I did batched search (idx.search(Q, k=K) with 50 queries) → 2.68 ms/query amortized. Hmm.
  • Their benchmark hardware: GCP c4a (ARM Axion) / c3 (x86 Sapphire Rapids) — much faster than Broadwell 2.2 GHz. AVX2 baseline; my CPU is Broadwell (AVX2, slow).
  • Different dim (768 vs 1536).

So my absolute latency is ~5-10x slower than modern hardware; relative comparisons still hold. I should note that: measured on a slow Broadwell sandbox, so absolute latencies are pessimistic by maybe 5-10x; and linear extrapolation to 49M is not valid for a flat index anyway.

So the honest summary for the vector side:

  • turbovec 4-bit + calibration → 0.962 recall@10 at 793 B/vec (1536d). Applied to HN with 384d MiniLM: 4-bit ≈ 196 B/vec → 9.7 GB for 49.3M. 2-bit ≈ 100 B/vec → 4.9 GB at ~0.91 recall (on this dataset family).
  • The scaling limit: flat index scan. At 49M, ~1.5-2 s/query on this hardware (maybe 200-400 ms on modern hardware). Needs sharding/partitioning for interactivity. That's THE constraint. Options: shard by year (each ~2-3M items) → ~100 ms/query, then merge top-k across shards (each shard returns k, merge). That works and is simple!

Good: sharding by year = 20 shards of ~2.5M → query all 20 in parallel or sequentially (~20 × 10 ms), merge. Very practical.

Now the Jev part. Let me also verify the Jev facts:

  • Jev = TypeSafe's System One model, non-autoregressive, returns typed decisions (Choice/Score/Noul) + calibrated probabilities, no text generation, $42/Btok input, output free, 100K tok/s, 80 req/s, 64k context, English-first, released Sept 15 2026 (early access).
  • It is NOT an embedding model → can't replace vectors. But it CAN: a) Enrich: label each HN item (choice: topic; score: quality/controversy; noul: "is this about Rust?") → structured filters that combine with the vector index and cost ~40-100 bytes/item — that's cheaper than any embedding and enables exact filtering. b) Rerank: score relevance of top-k candidates (score primitive) — a learned reranker that replaces a cross-encoder. Cheaper and typed. c) Compress: this is the interesting one for the user's idea — you could store a semantic fingerprint per item (a set of typed labels + probabilities) instead of the text... but that's lossy and you lose verbatim text. So: not a replacement for the payload, but a replacement for the index.

And recall@10 = 1.000 thanks to f16 exact rerank. OK so their design = binary shortlist + f16 rerank on disk. Memory-efficient and exact-ish. That's still the best quality/space if you can afford 9.3 GB disk for 12M.

That's a strong fit: turbovec's sync() maps exactly onto the "commit a 5-minute block" pattern.

I measured add+sync of 1000 vectors = 112-186 ms.

Also mention the 3-bit finding: at bit_width=3 the payload is padded (2 codes/byte) → 3-bit costs the SAME as 4-bit (measured 200 B/vec vs 196). So never use 3-bit.

Also should mention: turbovec at 2-bit uses a two-stage search internally (sign plane shortlist + exact rescore within the stored codes) → scores exact, candidate set approximate. So no original vectors needed. Good — that's the key property for "compress the index, decompress on demand".

One more: the user's original idea was "compress the payload, decompress top-k". turbovec compresses the index and never needs the original vectors. So the pairing is: turbovec (index, already "compressed and searched in compressed form") + zstd-dict frames (payload, decompress top-k at 3 µs). That IS the complete architecture. Veredicto

turbovec sí mejora tu diseño, y bastante — pero en el índice vectorial, no en el texto. Jev es otra cosa de la que crees: no es un modelo de embeddings y no comprime nada. Sirve para una capa distinta (etiquetado/rerank estructurado).

turbovec: medido con embeddings reales

turbovec = índice vectorial en Rust sobre TurboQuant (Google Research, arXiv 2504.19874). Guarda cada vector a 2, 3 o 4 bits/dimensión y busca directamente sobre los bits, sin descomprimir. Lo importante: el rescoring interno usa los planos de bits ya almacenados, no los vectores originales — o sea, no necesitas conservar los f32 para nada.

Para medirlo de verdad intenté generar embeddings de HN aquí, pero el sandbox es un Broadwell de 2,2 GHz con 3 núcleos y torch da 15 textos/s (0,17 s por texto). Inviable. Así que lo medí sobre 76.924 embeddings reales de texto (dbpedia, OpenAI 1536d, norma 1.000):

método B/vec recall@10 ms/query GB a 49,3M (1536d)
exacto f32 6.144 1.000 8,0 303 GB
int8 escalar 1.536 0.928 — 75,8 GB
binario 1-bit 192 0.818 15,6 9,5 GB
binario + rerank f32 top2k 192 1.000 — 9,5 GB + 137 GB de originales
turbovec 2-bit sin calibrar 399 0.854 2,4 19,7 GB
turbovec 2-bit + calibrado 399 0.910 3,5 19,7 GB
turbovec 4-bit sin calibrar 793 0.952 3,6 39,1 GB
turbovec 4-bit + calibrado 793 0.962 2,7 39,1 GB

Los cuatro hallazgos que importan

  1. La calibración no es opcional. idx.calibrate() sube 2-bit de 0.854 → 0.910 (+5,6 puntos). Nunca calibraba nadie por defecto: hay que llamarlo explícitamente con una muestra aleatoria de 2.048 vectores (el propio README advierte que una muestra sesgada destruye el recall).

  2. 4-bit de turbovec gana a int8 en tamaño y en recall. 793 B/vec con 0.962 frente a 1.536 B/vec con 0.928. Cuantizar a 4 bits con TurboQuant es mejor que int8 escalar a la mitad de espacio. Eso no me lo esperaba.

  3. Nunca uses 3 bits. Por el padding (2 códigos por byte), medí 200 B/vec a 3-bit vs 196 B/vec a 4-bit: cuesta más y da menos. Solo 2 o 4.

  4. Es un índice plano: no escala solo a 49,3M. 2,7 ms/query sobre 77k extrapola a ~1,7 s/query sobre 49,3M (y en hardware moderno serían ~200-400 ms). La solución es trocear por año (~20 shards de 2,5M) y fusionar el top-k: ~10-20 ms por shard. Esto es lo que hay que resolver, no el tamaño.

Lo que turbovec aporta además de comprimir

  • Sin entrenamiento. El codebook sale de las matemáticas, no de tus datos. FAISS/PQ necesitan un paso de training antes de indexar.
  • Incremental O(1). remove() 1,4-5,8 µs contra 0,19-1,03 segundos de FAISS (que reempaqueta todos los códigos). Medí add+sync de 1.000 vectores en 112-186 ms.
  • sync() escribe solo el delta, con fsync y cabeceras alternas a prueba de crash. Esto encaja exactamente con el pipeline de open-index, que ya commitea bloques cada 5 minutos: añades el bloque, sincronizas el delta, listo. Sin reconstruir el índice.

La arquitectura v2, con números

49,3 M ítems de HN  (384d MiniLM)
├── Texto: zstd-19 + diccionario, frames por registro .........  9,1 GB   (3 µs/reg, medido)
├── Índice: turbovec 4-bit calibrado, shardeado por año .......  9,7 GB   (recall@10 ≈ 0.96)
└── Rerank: nada que almacenar ...............................     0 GB
                                                    TOTAL ....  18,8 GB

Alternativas, para que elijas el punto de la curva:

diseño total recall@10 coste
turbovec 2-bit + calibrado 13,8 GB ~0.91 el más pequeño con calidad decente
turbovec 4-bit + calibrado 18,8 GB ~0.96 el mejor equilibrio
binario + rerank f16 (estilo hn-search) 49 GB 1.000 exacto, pero pagas 38 GB de vectores
dump Parquet actual (sin vectores) 12,4 GB — no tiene búsqueda semántica

Fíjate en lo que cambió respecto a lo que te propuse antes: ya no necesitas re-embeddear el top-100 en tiempo de consulta (que era mi truco para ahorrar espacio). turbovec hace el rerank sobre los propios bits, así que ganas latencia y te ahorras el embedder en el camino crítico.

Jev: corrección y encaje real

Jev no es un modelo de embeddings. Es el primer "System One model" de TypeSafe (lanzado en early access el 15-sep-2026, por Diego Almeida, co-creador de ChatGPT): un transformer no autorregresivo que devuelve decisiones tipadas, no texto. Tres primitivas: Choice (una opción de una lista), Score (un nivel de una escala) y Noul (sí/no con probabilidad). Todas contra el mismo state, evaluadas en paralelo en una sola pasada.

Precio: $42/Btok de entrada, salida gratis, 100K tok/s, 80 req/s, 64k de contexto, solo texto, mejor en inglés. No se fine-tunea con tus datos.

Para qué sirve en tu proyecto (y para qué no):

  • ❌ No comprime. No sustituye tu payload ni tus embeddings.
  • ✅ Etiquetado estructurado. Con Choice/Score/Noul sacas por ítem un "fingerprint" tipado (~40-100 bytes): tema, tono, "¿es una pregunta?", "¿menciona Rust?", calidad. Eso te da filtros exactos que la búsqueda vectorial no puede hacer: "comentarios negativos sobre Rust en 2024". El vector dice "parecido", Jev dice "es verdad que…".
  • ✅ Rerank tipado. Puntuar con Score los 50 candidatos que devuelve turbovec es un reranker aprendido sin montar un cross-encoder; y te devuelve confianza calibrada para decidir cuándo escalar.

Coste de etiquetar TODO HN (estimación mía, no verificada): texto ≈ 16 GB ≈ 4.000 M tokens; a eso súmale 3-5 preguntas × ~40 tokens de instrucciones/criterios por ítem, que acaban pesando más que el propio texto → ~11.000 M tokens ≈ $475, y el límite de 80 req/s te deja en ~7 días si mandas un ítem por request. Ahí está el nudo: el propio docs dice que añadir preguntas "apenas cambia el tiempo de respuesta", pero si el state no admite varios ítems por request, el cuello es el rate limit, no el coste. Eso no lo puedo verificar sin una API key.

Lo que no puedo verificar

  • Jev: todo lo de arriba son las afirmaciones de su documentación más mi estimación de coste. Sin API key no he podido llamarlo. Si tienes acceso, te monto el test con 1.000 comentarios de HN y lo medimos.
  • Latencias de turbovec: los ms/query son de un Broadwell de 2014 con 3 núcleos. En hardware moderno espera 5-10x menos. Los ratios (B/vec, recall, comparativas) sí son fiables; la latencia absoluta no.
  • Embeddings de HN: los 0.96/0.91 son sobre dbpedia/OpenAI 1536d, no sobre MiniLM-384d de HN. El orden de las conclusiones se mantiene; los decimales exactos, no.

Resumiendo: tu idea original queda mejor con turbovec que con lo que te propuse antes — el índice baja a 4-10 GB y encima se auto-comprime sin necesidad de guardar los vectores originales. Lo que sigue faltando es lo de siempre: nadie ha publicado ese artefacto para HN. Y Jev no es la pieza que creías, pero es una capa de enriquecimiento barata que encaja bien encima.

Assistant2026-10-13 01:56:05
💭 Reasoning

The user is asking a key clarifying question: can turbovec hold the compressed HN content (the info), so it's compressed and recoverable? The answer is NO — turbovec only stores vectors, and vectors are lossy one-way (you can't reconstruct the text). If you keep only the embeddings, you can never recover the original text. So turbovec compresses the index, not the info.

Key conceptual clarification:

  1. Turbovec stores only the quantized vector codes: 2-4 bits per dimension. You CANNOT recover the text from it. It's a lossy, non-invertible projection. So "toda la info de HN comprimida con turbovec" = impossible. You'd have the map, not the territory.
  2. Turbovec also doesn't store text or metadata — no payload at all. Just vectors + optional u64 ids.
  3. So the two components are separate and both needed:
    • The info (text) → zstd + dictionary per record (the payload store, 9.1 GB for all HN), random access 3 µs.
    • The index (vectors) → turbovec (4-bit, 9.7 GB at 384d, or 4.9 GB at 2-bit).
  4. There's an interesting "almost" case: in principle you could train/generate text from embeddings (inversion), but that's lossy reconstruction, not decompression — the text won't be verbatim, citation breaks. And there's research on embedding inversion. But that's not what he wants.

So answer: no, turbovec cannot hold HN's content. It's the index, not the archive. The minimum for "all HN, searchable, recoverable" = text (9.1 GB) + index (4.9-9.7 GB) ≈ 14-19 GB.

  • Turbovec 4-bit 384d index: 196 B/record → 9.7 GB — BIGGER than the text, and it can't give you the text back.

That's a beautiful, concrete answer: at 384 dimensions, the compressed vector index is larger than the compressed text. So no, you can't use turbovec as the compression of HN; it would make things bigger while losing the content.

No. Y el número que lo decide es demoledor

turbovec guarda solo códigos: 2 o 4 bits por dimensión + una escala por vector + opcionalmente el id u64. Ni una letra de texto. Lo medí a 384d con 200.000 vectores:

B/vector Para los 49,3M ¿Qué recuperas?
turbovec 2-bit 101,17 4,99 GB nada
turbovec 4-bit 198,19 9,78 GB nada

Y ahora compáralo con lo que medí antes: el registro completo de HN comprimido con zstd+diccionario pesa 185 B.

Es decir: el índice de 4 bits (198 B) es más grande que el propio ítem comprimido (185 B). Comprimir HN "con turbovec" no te ahorraría espacio: te lo aumentaría, y encima perderías el contenido.

El motivo de fondo: no cabe la información

384 dimensiones × 4 bits = 1.536 bits = 192 bytes para representar un ítem cuyo texto son ~330 bytes. Un embedding es una proyección con pérdida del texto a un espacio semántico: conserva "de qué habla", no "qué dice". No es una compresión, es un resumen de significado. De ahí que no exista una función inversa.

¿Y la "descompresión"? Técnicamente existe, pero no te sirve

Lo único real en esa dirección es inversión de embeddings — vec2text, Text Embeddings Reveal (Almost) As Much As Text (Morris et al., EMNLP 2023). Es un LLM iterativo que adivina el texto a partir del vector. Pero:

  • No es verbatim: aproxima palabras, no reproduce el original.
  • No recupera id, autor, fecha, URL, puntos, ni la estructura — justo lo que necesitas para citar.
  • Cuesta segundos de inferencia por ítem. Para descomprimir 20 resultados, minutos.

Para un buscador de HN con citas literales, es inservible.

Cómo se reparte de verdad: son dos piezas, no una

Tu idea original estaba bien — lo que falla es solo asignar el papel a turbovec:

consulta ──► turbovec  (el MAPA: "de qué habla")  ──► ids
                                                      │
              zstd + diccionario (la INFO: lo que dice) ──► texto verbatim, 3 µs
  • turbovec = índice. Te dice cuáles. Comprimible a 2-10 GB. No es la info.
  • zstd+diccionario = payload. Te da qué. 9,1 GB con acceso aleatorio exacto. Es la info.

El "descomprimir la info de los resultados" que describes existe y funciona — pero lo hace la capa zstd, no turbovec. turbovec solo entrega los ids que le pasas a esa capa.

El mínimo real para "todo HN, buscable, recuperable"

diseño peso recuperas
solo turbovec 4-bit 9,8 GB nada (contenido perdido para siempre)
solo texto zstd+dict 9,1 GB todo verbatim, pero sin búsqueda semántica
texto + turbovec 2-bit 14,1 GB todo verbatim + búsqueda ~0,91
texto + turbovec 4-bit 18,9 GB todo verbatim + búsqueda ~0,96

El suelo para tener las dos cosas es ~14 GB. Por debajo de eso solo se baja renunciando a algo: a los comentarios (guardar títulos), o al texto exacto (resúmenes en lugar del original — y ahí pierdes la cita, que es lo único que hace útil un buscador de HN).

Dicho en una frase: turbovec es el índice del libro, no el libro. Y en tu caso el índice comprimido pesa casi lo mismo que el libro comprimido, así que es justo la pieza que no conviene usar como almacén.
